readout switches in one run; UD-104 (~~#372~~) has a separate clock's counts at a
departure write and an arrival write on the uncoupled product arena; ~~#200~~, ~~#202~~,
~~#270~~, ~~#274~~ and ~~#278~~ have emission and return records with a clock reading under
their own laws. By the test of §6: the rate window is chosen (UA-4), and the write follows
from the occupancy bounds of UD-118 and the crossing lemma of UD-89; the count is
UD-123's. They pass no gate: the count is not tied to the record. UD-125 uses the assumptions
of UD-118. UD-126 uses in addition those of UD-123. UA-6 is not used.

Exact scope of UD-127 and UD-128 (BACKLOG ~~#383~~). What they give. A second record in the run
of UD-126, from the far pointer already on the arena: with its rate in a stated window its
readout is written during the interval of UD-118 on which the far half's occupancy is high.
So one run has two records in order, and the clock's own pointer has a count at a flow time
by which each is complete. The difference of the counts, times the period, is within two
periods of the difference of those flow times. What they do not give. (i) A radar time. There
is no emission record, and the two records are read on the one messenger's ring; there is no
reflector, no distance and no second observer. (ii) Certified events. Both writes are
thresholds of accumulated occupancy; carriers with suitable constant occupancies would give
readouts with the same clauses (a calculation, not in Lean; not exhibited on this arena).
(iii) Sharp times. `τ_a` and `τ_r` are the ends of the stated write intervals, not the
instants the readouts turn, and each is located only within its interval; so `τ_r − τ_a` is
known only to lie between `2500 n² δ` and `1250 n² (2π − 2δ)`. The far readout is shown to
display only through its window `1/(3κ₂)` after `τ_a`; it is not shown to display still when
the near readout is written. (iv) Time content beyond the
clock's. The two-time bound is UD-92 and holds for any two non-negative flow times; no
hypothesis relates the period to `τ_r − τ_a`, and both counts may be zero. (v) Counts read by
the records, or held by a degree of freedom: UO-14 (b). (vi) The limits of UD-123 and UD-126,
with the gated pointer free and not shown to tick; the writes and counts are `IsRecordWrite`
and `IsTickCount` statements about readout histories, not `ActualOccurrence`s. (vii) That
the four rate hypotheses hold for some `n` in Lean: each pair is compatible for `δ ≤ π/48`
and `fineEta n δ ≤ 1/48`. Each rate is `−2 g f'` at its fibre and must be positive; the round
fibres bound `|g₁ f(θ₂)| + |g₂ h(ψ₂)|` on the same fibres. That one fibre pair meets both
windows, both signs and the round-fibre bound is not stated in Lean. Earlier: BACKLOG
~~#360~~, ~~#366~~ and ~~#369~~ have counts at readout switches in one run; UD-94 (~~#367~~)
has a departure write and an arrival write in one run (thresholds of accumulated weight) and
UD-104 (~~#372~~) a separate clock's counts at both, for the crossing packet on other
arenas. ~~#200~~ and ~~#202~~ (echo arena), ~~#270~~ (reflection, stated detector law) and ~~#274~~ and ~~#278~~ (relay, stated hybrid law) have emission and return records with a clock
reading, under their own laws. What is new is the pair on the free round trip, with the clock a tensor factor of the
same carrier. By the test of §6: the two rate windows are chosen (UA-4); the writes follow
from the occupancy bounds of UD-118 and the crossing lemma of UD-89; the counts are UD-123's
and the bound is UD-92. They pass no gate: neither count is tied to a record. UD-127 uses the
assumptions of UD-118. UD-128 uses in addition those of UD-123. UA-6 is not used.

Exact scope of UD-129 and UD-130 (BACKLOG ~~#384~~). What they give. A third record in a run
like that of UD-128 on the variant arena of UA-17 (f), written during the first presence, from a second pointer on the near half with a
larger rate. So one run has three records in order (`τ_d ≤ τ_a ≤ τ_r`), before, during and
after the absence, and
the clock's own pointer has a count at a flow time by which each is complete. The count
differences from the first, times the period, are within two periods of the differences of
the flow times. The midpoint of the first and last flow times lies in the absence, and half
the sum of their counts, times the period, is within one period of it. What they do not give.
(i) An emission record. The messenger is prepared in the near half and the third pointer
accumulates its presence there; nothing is emitted and no departure is recorded. (ii)
Certified events. All three writes are thresholds of accumulated occupancy; carriers with
suitable constant occupancies would give readouts with the same clauses (a calculation, not
in Lean; not exhibited on this arena). (iii) Sharp times. `τ_d`, `τ_a`, `τ_r` are the ends of
the stated write intervals, not the instants the readouts turn, and each is located only
within its interval. Each readout is shown to display only through its window after its `τ`;
the three are not shown to display at one flow time, and the third pointer keeps turning
whenever the messenger is in the near half. (iv) A radar time. The midpoint clause is
arithmetic on the three intervals. The first presence ends before the absence and the return leg begins after it, and for
`δ ≤ π/2` the midpoint of any non-negative time up to `roundOut` and any time of the return
leg lies in the absence. The two intervals are not symmetric about it: the return leg is
twice as long as the first presence. Interval arithmetic puts the midpoint in
`[1250 n² (3π/4 + δ/2), 1250 n² (3π/2 − δ)]`; the theorem states only the absence's whole
interval. It locates the midpoint only to within the absence's whole
length `1250 n² (π − 2δ)`, the interval that also holds `τ_a`. It is formed from flow times;
no degree of freedom holds it. There is no reflector and no distance, the messenger returns
because the ring closes, and there is no second observer. (v) Time content beyond the
clock's. The bounds are UD-92's and hold at any non-negative flow times; no hypothesis
relates the period to any interval, and all three counts may be zero. (vi) Counts read by the
records, or held by a degree of freedom: UO-14 (b). (vii) The gated pointer. It is absent
from this variant (UA-17 (f)), so UD-113 to UD-116, UD-119 to UD-121 and the gated-count
statements of UD-124, which are for the level swap, say nothing about this run. The generic
lemmas of UD-111 (`gateFlow_prodRay`, `gateShift`) are used here with `X = g₃ · 1`. No term
couples the messenger and the clock: neither the product coupling nor the composite carrier
is exercised, and from a product ray the run is that of UD-104's uncoupled arena with a third
region pointer (a calculation, not in Lean). (viii) That the hypotheses hold together in
Lean: six rate windows and three signs. The first two pairs are those of UD-128; the third is
satisfiable with `T₁ = 0` by any large enough rate once `δ < π/2` and `fineEta n δ < 1`, which
the first two windows force, and a positive `T₁` only up to
`(1 − fineEta n δ)/2 · roundOut n δ` (calculations, not in Lean); at `δ = π/2` the theorem is
vacuous. That one start meets all of them with the
round-fibre bound is not stated. (ix) More than null sets: the third and fourth profiles
vanish on their fibres and the start is a product ray (calculations, not in Lean). The writes
and counts are `IsRecordWrite` and `IsTickCount` statements about readout histories, not
`ActualOccurrence`s. Earlier: UD-94 (~~#367~~) has a departure write and an arrival write in
one run (thresholds of accumulated weight); BACKLOG ~~#369~~ three records and two counts in
one run, for one momentum mode; UD-104 (~~#372~~) a separate clock's counts at two writes;
~~#200~~ and ~~#202~~ (echo arena), ~~#270~~ (reflection, stated detector law) and ~~#274~~
and ~~#278~~ (relay, stated hybrid law) have emission and return records with a clock
reading, under their own laws. What is new is the third write, during the first presence, in a
round-trip run like that of UD-128, and the midpoint clause. By the test of §6: the
third pointer, its operator and the three rate windows are chosen (UA-17 (f), UA-4); the
writes follow from the occupancy bounds of UD-118 and the crossing lemma of UD-89; the counts
are the swing of UD-123 on this variant and the bounds are UD-92's; the midpoint is
arithmetic. They pass no gate: no count is tied to a record. UD-129 and UD-130 use UA-17 with
(f), UA-15, UA-13, UA-12, UA-8, UA-7, UA-5 and UA-4; UA-2 enters only through the invariance
statement. UA-6 is not used.

Exact scope of UD-131 and UD-132 (BACKLOG ~~#385~~). What they give. Every write since UD-89
is a threshold of accumulated occupancy, and a carrier with a suitable constant occupancy
would give one such readout. UD-131 shows that two readouts taken together exclude that
under a rate condition (where the condition fails the caveat stands, a calculation): if
two ready pointers are turned by one function at known rates from known start angles, the fast
one displays by `T_o` and the slow one not before `T_b`, and the rate condition holds, the
function's mean over `[T_o, T_b]` is strictly less than over `[0, T_o]`, so it is not
non-decreasing and not constant. UD-132 is that statement for the third and first readouts of
the run of UD-130, for every function that reproduces those two histories on
`[0, roundBack]`. What they do not
give. (i) A departure. The conclusion compares two means; it has no direction, path or speed
and does not locate the drop within `[0, T_b]`. (ii) A return: nothing shows the mean rising
again. (iii) A record of the conclusion. The premises are readout histories over intervals of
flow time (UA-7), "does not display at any flow time before `T_b`" among them; no record
holds them and no degree of freedom holds the conclusion. (iv) The form of the drive. That
both pointers are turned by the accumulated value of one function at known rates is assumed
(UA-5, UA-13); the rates, the start angles and the arcs are apparatus input (UA-4). The
theorem does not identify `F` with an occupancy. In the run the coupled carrier's near-half
occupancy is one such `F` (`threeRecord_nearOcc_drop`, by the readout lemmas of UD-130). For it
the drop also follows without the rate condition from the occupancy bounds of UD-126 and UD-129
(built on UD-118), for a small margin and a small `fineEta`, by a calculation not stated in
Lean. What is new is that the drop follows from
the two histories, the rates, the start angles and the rate condition, for every continuous
non-negative `F` that reproduces both histories on `[0, T_b]`; in particular no constant `F`
reproduces them. (v)
A certificate from one readout: each single write still certifies nothing. (vi) That the rate
condition holds: it is a hypothesis, and the rate windows of UD-130 do not imply it (it bounds
the third rate from above; with `T₁ = 0` the windows bound it only from below). With equal start
angles, the third rate at the least value of its window and the first at the greatest (the corner
most favourable to the condition; elsewhere it is stronger, and it fails for a large third rate)
it reads `2 · roundUpper ≤ (1 − fineEta) · roundBack`. That is weaker than the compatibility `2 ·
roundUpper ≤ roundLower` of the first pointer's window (UD-126) and is proved from it for `δ ≤
π/48` and `fineEta n δ ≤ 1/48`; that all the conditions hold together for some start is otherwise
not stated in Lean. (vii) More than the run of UD-130, with its limits: no term
couples the messenger and the clock there, and the clock plays no part in UD-132 (its
hypotheses are carried because the writes are taken from UD-130); null sets of fibres and
starts. That the generic statement applies off those null sets is expected, not in Lean.
Earlier: UD-105 (~~#373~~) has a transit certificate in accumulated occupancies with no
pointer in it. By the test of §6: the two rates, the start angles and the rate condition are
chosen (UA-4); the drop follows from the two histories by the intermediate value theorem and
the arcs. They pass no gate: nothing here is a record of a departure, and UO-16 is not met.
UD-131 uses UA-4 only, as the arcs. UD-132 uses in addition the assumptions of UD-130. UA-6
is not used.

Exact scope of UD-133 and UD-134 (BACKLOG ~~#386~~). What they give. UD-131 and UD-132 read a
drop and said nothing about the return leg. UD-133 reads a rise from one pointer on two arcs:
if a ready pointer turned by one function at a known rate does not display before `T_b`, is
still in the ready arc at `T_b`, and displays by `T_e`, the function accumulates more over
`[T_b, T_e]` than over `[0, T_b]`; when `[T_b, T_e]` is no longer than `[T_o, T_b]` its mean
over the later interval is strictly greater, so it is not non-increasing and not constant.
UD-134 is that statement for the first pointer of the run of UD-130, and with UD-132 a dip:
the mean over `[roundOut, roundBack]` is strictly less than the mean before and the mean
after. What they do not give. (i) A return. The conclusions compare accumulated values and
means over stated intervals of flow time; they have no direction, path or speed, do not
locate the rise within the return leg, and do not say that what the pointer registers there
is what was there before. The dip is three means, not a departure and a return, and no radar
time is formed from it. (ii) A record of the conclusion. The premises are a display history
over an interval and an arc membership at one flow time (UA-7); no record holds them and no
degree of freedom holds the conclusion. (iii) A certificate from one display history: the
rise uses one more premise about the same pointer, its ready arc at the flow time `T_b`.
The threshold writes of the region pointers have read only the displayed arc after the
start; tick records have read a ready arc at later flow times since ~~#358~~, but not this
pointer's. Reading the first region pointer's ready arc at `roundBack` is a further
apparatus input, now recorded in UA-4. (iv) The form of the drive: assumed
(UA-5, UA-13), as in UD-131; `F` is not identified with an occupancy. In the run the coupled
carrier's near-half occupancy is one such `F` (`threeRecord_nearOcc_dip`); for it the rise
also follows from the occupancy bounds of UD-126 for a small margin and a small `fineEta`,
by a calculation not stated in Lean. (v)
That the hypotheses can be met. The rise needs `κ₁ · roundUpper ≤ 1/6 − a_θ` in place of
UD-130's `κ₁ · roundUpper ≤ 1/6`; with `1/3 ≤ κ₁ · roundLower` that asks
`roundLower / roundUpper ≥ 2/(1 − 6 a_θ)`, and the proved ratio `5/2` (for `δ ≤ π/96`,
`fineEta n δ ≤ 1/96`) is enough for `a_θ ≤ 1/30` (a calculation). The ratio is below `3`
for every `n > 0` and `δ > 0`, so for a start angle `a_θ ≥ 1/18` the two conditions cannot
both hold and UD-134 is vacuous there (a calculation). The dip needs in addition
the rate condition of UD-132, which the rate windows do not imply; the tightened window
works against it (with equal start angles `a` its most favourable corner is
`2 · roundUpper ≤ (1 − fineEta)(1 − 6a) · roundBack`, not covered by
`two_roundUpper_le_roundBack`; a calculation). That one start meets all
of them is not stated in these rows (UD-136 exhibits one); `threeRecord_nearOcc_dip` shows only that the class of
functions is not empty when they are met. (vi) More than the run of UD-130, with its limits:
no term couples the messenger and the clock there, the clock plays no part in UD-134 and
the far and third pointers none in its rise (their hypotheses are carried only), null
sets of fibres and starts. That the generic statement applies off those null sets is
expected, not in Lean. Earlier: UD-131 and UD-132 (the drop, from two pointers); UD-105
(~~#373~~). By the test of §6: the rate, the start angle, the arcs and the tightened window
are chosen (UA-4); the rise follows from the readouts by the intermediate value theorem and
the arcs. They pass no gate: nothing here is a record of a return, and UO-16 is not met.
UD-133 uses UA-4 only, as the arcs. UD-134 uses in addition the assumptions of UD-130. UA-6
is not used.

Exact scope of UD-135 and UD-136 (BACKLOG ~~#387~~). What they give. UD-126 to UD-134 carried
rate windows, a round-fibre bound, and for UD-132 and UD-134 a rate condition and a tightened
window, as hypotheses, with no start shown to meet them together. UD-135 and UD-136 exhibit
one: the profile `trainF` on all three region pointers, each started at moving angle `1/30`
on the fibre `0`, with strengths that are the reciprocals of six times the occupancy bounds.
The hypotheses of UD-130 (at `T₁ = 0`), UD-132 and UD-134 then hold for every margin
`0 < δ ≤ π/96` and index with `fineEta n δ ≤ 1/96`, and at `δ = π/96` every `n ≥ 73744` is
such an index. What they do not give. (i) Anything beyond satisfiability. The conclusions are
those of UD-130, UD-132 and UD-134 with all their limits: threshold writes that certify
nothing, counts read at flow times that may be zero, a dip of means, premises that are
readouts at flow times held by no record, no radar time. (ii) Positive measure. The pointers
start at one point; the windows are met with equality at their lower ends, so the instance
is at the edge of each window; the carrier's start is one product ray and the clock's one
start. (iii) Back-reaction. On the fibres `0` all three profiles vanish, so no region
pointer acts back on the messenger: it moves as the free packet, and the round-fibre bound
holds trivially. (iv) Untuned apparatus. The strengths depend on `n` and `δ` through the
occupancy bounds; they are chosen with the round trip's schedule known in flow time (UA-4).
(v) Physical numbers. The index bound is not sharp and with `640000 n³ ≤ M + 1` gives a ring
of more than `2 · 10²⁰` sites (a calculation). (vi) A closed statement about the records.
The three instance theorems keep the ring's length, the index bound and the clock's
parameters as hypotheses (each plainly satisfiable; not stated as one existential), and the
certificate keeps its premises about `F`. Only `exists_nearOcc_dip`, which involves no
pointer and no clock, has no hypothesis. By the test of §6: everything here is chosen
apparatus input (UA-4) checked against inequalities already proved; no assumption is
restated as a result, and nothing is derived about records beyond UD-130 to UD-134. They
pass no gate, and UO-16 is not met. UD-135 uses no assumption (real arithmetic). UD-136 uses
the assumptions of UD-130. UA-6 is not used.

Exact scope of UD-137 and UD-138 (BACKLOG ~~#388~~; step (2) of the plan under UO-16). What
they give. UD-64 has the tick law for a localised clock packet with a localisation error
`ε T²`, and says that no packet of positive width is shown to meet its conditions. On a fibre
where the pointer's profile vanishes, UD-137 writes the packet's accumulated reading exactly
as a sum over its modes, and UD-138 bounds the pointer's distance from a swing by a term
linear in the horizon. For the packet of UD-64's device with a chosen strength the four
threshold conditions then follow from one inequality, linear in `T`, and the count obeys
the dilated clock law with the errors of UD-64. What they do not give. (i) An observer. The
arena is the clock arena of UA-12: the clock carrier and its own pointer. No messenger, no
detector, no meeting; the coincidence detector of the author's decision of 2026-11-14 is not
built. (ii) An exhibited packet of positive width. `κ > 0`, the inequality and the lower
bound `ρ` of the rates are hypotheses in these two rows; UD-142 discharges them for narrow
windows. (iii) More than one fibre and one start: profile `trainF` on the fibre `0`,
where the pointer does not act back on the clock; off it the quadratic back-reaction term
of UD-64 remains. (iv) A recorded speed. `v` is the relativistic velocity at the reference
momentum; that the packet moves at it is not shown here, and no position of the clock is
recorded. (v) A count held against anything but the flow time (UA-7). (vi) A horizon shown
long enough for a radar exchange: that it is of order `1/ε`, within which the packet travels
of order `E/(μ₁ − μ₀)` of its own widths, is a calculation, not in Lean. By the test of §6:
the strength, the start and the fibre are chosen (UA-4); the dilation comes from the level
splitting's dependence on momentum (UD-58), not from a hypothesis on the clock's rate. They
pass no gate, and UO-16 is not met. UD-137 uses no assumption beyond the device's energy
(UA-12). UD-138 uses UA-4, UA-5, UA-7, UA-12. UA-6 is not used.

Exact scope of UD-141 and UD-142. What they give. UD-138 bounded every tick count of a clock
packet under three conditions that were hypotheses, and showed neither a packet of several
modes meeting them nor a tick. UD-141 adds that the count exists and is at least `n` once the
phase has reached `2π (n − 1) + π`. UD-142 discharges the three conditions, for speed `1`
and levels `1` and `1 + Δ`, for every window of modes within `1/(600 n)` of a reference
momentum in the lower part of the band, at the horizon `T = 2π n/R`; gives the bound as a
rate with error `1/n + Δ`; bounds `v = σ/E₀` below by `p₀/π`; and exhibits such windows of
any odd number of modes. Each tick is a passage of the pointer's history from its resting
set to its displaying set, a record in the sense of UD-62. What they do not give. (i) An
observer: as UD-138, the clock carrier and its own pointer only; what a moving observer is
in `Σ` is not settled here. (ii) More than one flow time: one count at `T`. (iii) Ticks
beyond the horizon: `n` ticks are shown for momentum width at most `1/(600 n)`; for a fixed
packet the number shown is bounded. (iv) Localisation in space: the window is a statement
about momenta. (v) A resolved dilation in these rows: `1/n + Δ` is to be compared with
`1 − √(1 − v²)`; UD-144 has an instance. (vi) Motion: `v` is `σ/E₀` at the reference
momentum; the band's group velocity is `v cos(a p₀) ≥ v/2` (a calculation, not in Lean);
that the packet moves is not shown, and no speed or position is recorded. (vii) The
strength: it exists; the statement does not record it; it is tuned to the packet (UA-4).
(viii) More than one fibre and one start. (ix) A count held against anything but the flow
time (UA-7); `T` is chosen with the rate known. By the test of §6: the dilation comes from
the level splitting's dependence on momentum (UD-58); no hypothesis states the clock's rate.
They pass no gate, and UO-16 is not met. Assumptions used: UA-4, UA-5, UA-7, UA-12. UA-6 is
not used.

Exact scope of UD-143 and UD-144. What they give. UD-142 said nothing about where the clock
carrier is. UD-143 carries the messenger's bound (`regionWeight_ringPacket_le`, BACKLOG
~~#265~~: weight far from the predicted direction is bounded by the translation phase) to
the two-level device, level by level. UD-144 evaluates it for one family that also ticks,
`N` flat modes, with the budget `1/N + 1/(20000 Δ n N)` at every packet time of the horizon
of `n` ticks; and shows for `n ≥ 400`, `Δ ≤ 1/400` that the count at the horizon is short of
the undilated count by more than its error: `2π N'/Δ ≤ (1 − 1/400) T`. What they do not
give. (i) A region: none is exhibited, the hypotheses hold for the empty one, and the bound
says something only when `1 − γ` exceeds the budget. (ii) A sharp position: arcs of
half-angle large against the square root of the budget only (`1/√N` when `20000 Δ n ≥ 1`),
while the packet's own width is of order `1/N` (a calculation). (iii) The travel: the
predicted angle over the horizon is `π v/(600 R N)`, about `0.003/(Δ N)`; that it exceeds
the resolved arc for `Δ √N` small against `0.002` and `n` large against `1/Δ` is a
calculation, not in Lean. (iv) An arena observable or a record of position: the statement is
about the uncoupled state vector at a packet time; that the flow's ray on the fibre where
the profile vanishes is that packet is not a named theorem. (v) Motion: in the resolved
dilation `v` is `σ/E₀ ≥ 1/(2π)`; that the packet moves at it is not shown. (vi) More than
one count at one flow time (UA-7). (vii) Anything off the fibre where the profile vanishes,
or with a second coupling present. (viii) An observer. They pass no gate, and UO-16 is not
met. Assumptions used: UA-12 (the device's energy); for the ticks, as UD-142.

Exact scope of UD-145 and UD-146. What they give. UD-140 had the couplings of UA-18 (c) as
operators. UD-145 puts two of them into one flow on the two-pointer arena of UA-13 over a
composite of two carriers, and solves it where neither pointer acts back. UD-146 reads the
gated displacement site by site and shows the structural fact behind UA-18 (d): a pointer
gated on "together" and one gated on "apart" move, between them, exactly as one ungated
pointer on the clock. What they do not give. (i) A record: no swing, tick, count or reading;
tick counts are not shown to add. (ii) Anything off the fibres where both profiles vanish,
or from a start that is not a product ray; both are null sets, and off them a gated term
joins the carriers. (iii) An instance: no messenger, clock, wall or coincidence relation is
put in; the carriers' energies are arbitrary Hermitian matrices. (iv) Three carriers or five
pointers. They pass no gate, and UO-16 is not met. Assumptions used: UA-13 (the two-pointer
arena), UA-17 (c) and UA-18 (c) (the form of the coupling).

Exact scope of UD-147 and UD-148 (BACKLOG ~~#394~~; step (4) of the plan under UO-16). What
they give. UA-18 (e) says a reading is the number of tick records a gated pointer wrote in its
first open period. UD-147 is that statement for one pointer's history: a count while the drive
is a swing, a bounded advance while the drive stops, and a count fixed up to one afterwards.
UD-148 derives the three phases for the flow of UD-145 from where the two carriers are: gate
open means little weight outside the relation, gate closed means little weight inside it.
Every one of the five readings of UA-18 (d) is to be an application of UD-148, to the gate or
to its complement. What they do not give. (i) An instance: the weight hypotheses and the
clock's swing are hypotheses; no messenger, clock packet, relation or region is put in and
the hypotheses are not shown to hold together for any. (ii) A sharp reading: between `t_a` and
`t_b` the count may advance by up to `6|z| (t_b − t_a) + 3/2`, which is small only if the
gate closes quickly against the pointer's speed. (iii) Anything after `t_c`. (iv) A record
write for a tick completed after `t_a`; up to `t_a` that is `IsSwing.isRecordWrite` of UD-61,
not restated. (v) A speed, a distance or a second observer: `t_a`, `t_b`, `t_c` and `R` are
in flow time. (vi) Three carriers, walls, or fibres where a pointer acts back. They pass no
gate, and UO-16 is not met. Assumptions used: UA-13, UA-17 (c), UA-18 (c) and (e); UA-12 for
the level swap.

Exact scope of UD-149 and UD-150 (BACKLOG ~~#395~~; step (4) of the plan under UO-16). What
they give. UD-148 reads a gated pointer under two hypotheses on where the carriers are. UD-149
and UD-150 prove both for a messenger packet and a clock packet on one ring, with the relation
"within the angle `w`" and an explicit bound: while the predicted angles are within `w − 2δ`
the weight outside the relation is small (the gate is open), and while they are more than
`w + 2δ` apart the weight inside it is small (the gate is closed). The bound is the two
packets' budgets over `1 − cos δ`. A pair is exhibited: the flat clock packet of UD-143 and a
flat messenger packet on the same ring. What they do not give. (i) A reading: UD-148 is not
applied. Its third hypothesis, the clock's swing with a margin, and the conditions that tie the
bound to the number of ticks are not discharged. (ii) Flow times: the conditions are on the
predicted angles at one flow time; no `t_a`, `t_b` is exhibited, and the interval between
"open" and "closed" is not bounded. By a calculation, not in Lean, it is `4δ` over the
difference of the angular velocities. (iii) The messenger of M6: the flat messenger has a rest
energy at least `1` at the clock's momentum, so it is no faster than the clock; the general
lemmas take any ring packet. (iv) A fine resolution: `δ` must be large against `1/√N`. (v)
Records: these are expectations along uncoupled orbits. (vi) Walls, a third carrier, or fibres
where a pointer acts back. They pass no gate, and UO-16 is not met. Assumptions used: UA-8
(the propagation law), UA-12, UA-18 (c).

Exact scope of UD-151 and UD-152 (BACKLOG ~~#396~~; step (4) of the plan under UO-16). What
they give. UD-148 had three hypotheses and UD-150 proved two. UD-151 proves the third: the
clock packet's own pointer displacement is within `1/24` of a swing of half-amplitude `1/4`,
which leaves a margin of `1/24` for the gate's leak. UD-152 applies UD-148: a gated pointer on
a messenger packet and the flat clock packet has a number of ticks that is one of two
consecutive numbers at every flow time of an interval during which the two are apart, within a
stated bound of `R t_a / 2π`. It is the first statement of this step that is a count of an
actual run of the coupled flow with carriers put in. What they do not give. (i) Values: the
conditions are inequalities between `n, N, Δ, w, δ, ε, t_a, t_b, t_c`, the messenger's budget
and the predicted angles, and no choice is shown to meet them together; that is deferred to
the messenger of M6. (ii) A messenger: none is exhibited, and its speed is not assumed. (iii)
An emission: on the ring the two start together and separate once; no event is recorded at
`t_a`, and the count is compared with the flow time `t_a` (UA-7). (iv) A sharp count: the term
`6 g (t_b − t_a)` is the closing of the gate. (v) Anything after `t_c`; a record write for a
tick after `t_a`. (vi) Walls, a third carrier, or fibres where a pointer acts back. They pass
no gate, and UO-16 is not met. Assumptions used: UA-4 (the strength is tuned to the clock
packet), UA-7, UA-8, UA-12, UA-13, UA-17 (c), UA-18 (c) and (e).

Exact scope of UD-153 and UD-154 (BACKLOG ~~#397~~; step (4) of the plan under UO-16). What
they give. UD-152 was conditional on inequalities between real numbers that no values were
shown to meet. UD-153 reduces them, for a light flat messenger, to four scalar conditions, and
UD-154 meets those along a family indexed by `k`: the messenger's velocity is within `1/(2k²)`
of `1`, the count is of at least about `47 k` ticks, and its relative difference from
`R t_a / 2π` is at most `15/k`. So the inequalities of UD-152 can be met, with a messenger
whose velocity tends to `1` and an error that tends to zero. What they do not give. (i) A
recorded speed: the messenger's velocity is `vRel` at its reference momentum and enters only
through the predicted angles where UD-150 shows its weight to be; no hypothesis states a
speed. (ii) An emission: on the ring the two packets start together and separate once;
nothing is recorded at `t_a`, which is a flow time (UA-7). (iii) Anything after
`(3/2) t_b`. (iv) One system: the ring has more than about `4 · 10²⁷ k¹¹` sites, the gap falls
as `k⁻⁶` and the flow times grow as `k⁷`; each `k` is a different generator. (v) Walls, a
third carrier, a second observer, or fibres where a pointer acts back; the values will not
carry over unchanged to the arena of UA-18. They pass no gate, and UO-16 is not met.
Assumptions used: as UD-152.

Exact scope of UD-155 and UD-156 (BACKLOG ~~#398~~; step (4) of the plan under UO-16). What
they give. Four of the five readings of UA-18 (d) belong to gates open from the flow time `0`
and are read by UD-148. The fifth, the moving observer's ticks while the messenger is not with
it, belongs to a gate that opens in the middle of the run. By UD-146 its pointer's
displacement is the clock's own minus the emission pointer's, so while the messenger is away
it is `(1/4)(1 − cos R t) − c`, `c` the displacement at which the emission pointer stopped.
UD-155 counts the ticks of any swing over a window. UD-156 shows that such an offset pointer
ticks once a turn when `c` is in one of two bands, and does not tick at all when it is in a
third. What they do not give. (i) The return reading: no flow, carrier or gate appears; the
gate that closes, opens and closes again is not assembled. (ii) Where `c` lies: while the
emission gate closes, `c` is bounded only by the pointer's speed (UD-147), so it is not shown
to lie in either band, nor in `[0, 1/2]`. (iii) Anything within `η` of the edges
`c = 1/4, 7/12, 11/12` (for both pointers started at `1/12`); there the count can differ from
the phase by up to one tick a turn, and no choice of arcs and amplitude removes the edges
(calculations, not in Lean). They pass no gate, and UO-16 is not met. ⚠️ *For the author:*
UA-18 (e) says each reading is the number of tick records in the pointer's first open period.
For the return reading that number follows the clock only for runs in which the emission
pointer stopped inside one of the two bands. The return reading, and with it M6, will carry
that as a hypothesis about the run unless the author decides otherwise.

Exact scope of UD-157 and UD-158 (BACKLOG ~~#399~~; step (4) of the plan under UO-16). What
they give. The return reading of UA-18 (d): the number of ticks of the pointer coupled to the
complementary gate, which is closed while the messenger is with the clock, open while it is
away and closed again when it is back. At every flow time of an interval after the return the
count is within a stated bound of `R (t₃ − t_b)/2π`, the time away in ticks; the bound has a
term for the closing of the emission gate and one for its reopening. UD-158 is the statement
for any messenger packet and the flat clock packet on one ring, with both gates in one run.
What they do not give. (i) An unconditional reading: the conclusion is for runs in which the
emission pointer's displacement at `t_b` is in one of the two bands of UD-156, and where that
displacement lies is not shown. (ii) Values: the conditions are inequalities between real
numbers; no run in which the messenger comes back is exhibited, and `t₃ ≤ 2π n/R` asks the
clock's horizon to cover the time away. (iii) How the messenger comes back: the hypotheses
are on predicted angles; on the ring a faster messenger laps the clock, with a wall it would
be reflected, and neither is used. (iv) The radar time and distance: the two counts are not
added or halved here. (v) Records at `t_a`, `t_b`, `t₃`, `t₄`, which are flow times. They pass
no gate, and UO-16 is not met. Assumptions used: as UD-152.

Exact scope of UD-159 and UD-160 (BACKLOG ~~#400~~; step (4) of the plan under UO-16). What
they give. A bound of where the emission pointer stops: within
`δ₀ + 2|z| ε t_a + 2|z| (t_b − t_a)` of `(1/4)(1 − cos R t_a)`, the clock's swing at the last
flow time at which the two carriers are certified together. With a finer swing of the clock
(`1/96` over a quarter of its horizon) this turns the band of UD-157 and UD-158 into two
conditions that do not mention the pointer: the gate closes within a small part of a tick,
and `1/6 < |cos R t_a|`. What they do not give. (i) The band removed: runs with
`|cos R t_a| ≤ 1/6` are not covered, and UD-156 shows that near `cos R t_a = 0` the count
need not follow the clock. (ii) A slowly closing gate: while the gate closes only the
pointer's speed is used, and the ring statement asks `2 (R/5) (t_b − t_a) ≤ 1/48`. In the
family of UD-154 the closing lasts many ticks, so that family does not meet the condition;
for such a run nothing is shown about where the pointer stops. (iii) A reading of the phase:
`R t_a` is the clock's rate times a flow time, a number of the arena; no pointer reads it.
(iv) Values, how the messenger comes back, the radar time and distance: as UD-157 and
UD-158. They pass no gate, and UO-16 is not met. Assumptions used: as UD-152. ⚠️ *For the
author:* the note under UD-155 and UD-156 stands, in this sharper form: the return reading
is now conditional on the clock's phase at closing and on a fast closing, not on where a
pointer stopped; whether a run that fails the phase condition is to be excluded, or the
arrangement changed so that no such condition is needed, is the author's decision. *Settled
2026-11-15 by the charter, see the note under UO-16: the condition is an inequality on the
prepared parameters (UA-18 (b), UA-4); M6 must exhibit values meeting it and say that
outside it this arrangement gives no reading.*

Exact scope of UD-161 and UD-162 (BACKLOG ~~#401~~; step (4) of the plan under UO-16). What
they give. Both pointers of the run with both gates are read: the first pointer's count `N_e`
at a flow time while the messenger is away, the last pointer's count `N_r` at a flow time
after it is back. With `e = N_e` and `r = N_e + N_r`, the two readings of
`radar_lorentz_error` in ticks for one observer, `(e + r)/2` is within a stated number of
ticks of `R (t_a + (t₃ − t_b)/2)/2π` and `(r − e)/2` of `R (t₃ − t_b)/(4π)`. What they do not
give. (i) Two readings held together: after `t₄` the first pointer's gate is open again and
it ticks on, so at the flow times at which `N_r` is read it no longer shows `N_e`; no pointer
holds a first-period count after the period ends, and nothing says the two are read
together. (ii) A pointer that shows `(e + r)/2` or `(r − e)/2`: they are arithmetic in the
statement. (iii) A distance in sites: `(r − e)/2` is half the time away in ticks; that it is
the distance to where the messenger turned is not stated, how it comes back is not said, and
its speed enters no hypothesis. (iv) The closing and reopening of the gate: neither count
sees `[t_a, t_b]` or `[t₃, t₄]`; they are error terms. (v) Values, and anything for runs
outside the conditions of UD-159 and UD-160. They pass no gate, and UO-16 is not met.
Assumptions used: as UD-152.

Exact scope of UD-163 and UD-164 (BACKLOG ~~#402~~; the plan under UO-16 restated from the
top). What they give. M6 written once as a conditional theorem about one run: five pointers
read as in UA-18 (d) and (e), their counts in the unit `2π/R₀` fed to
`radar_lorentz_error`, the conclusion its Lorentz statement with errors read off the five
histories. Nothing is hidden: what is not proved is a hypothesis of the theorem. What they
do not give, and this is the list of what M6 still needs, each as a theorem about records of
one flow on the arena of UA-18 with values exhibited. (R1) The five histories, for the
five-pointer flow from a product of the three carriers; for two carriers and two pointers
the emission and return histories follow from where the carriers are (UD-148, UD-157,
UD-159), for three carriers and five pointers nothing is proved. (R2) Where the carriers
are: packets on the chain with two walls, the reflection, `A` at rest, `B` moving. (R3) The
two relations between the flow times, which state the messenger's speed and `B`'s velocity;
by the test of UO-16 they may not stay hypotheses and are to come from the packets' band
velocities. (R4) The moving clock's rate `√(1 − v²) R₀`; by the same test it is to come
from UD-138. (R5) The band of the return pointer, or the conditions of UD-159 that imply it.
(R6) Values meeting all the inequalities together. (R7) An open set of the pointers'
conserved angles (UA-18 (g)). They pass no gate, and UO-16 is not met. Assumptions used:
none beyond the definition of the tick count (UD-62); the theorem is on no arena.

Scoping of R2 to R4 against what exists (2026-11-15; reading, no new Lean). The theorem was
first written with the two relations between the gates' closing times and with the rate
exactly `√(1 − v²) R₀`; neither can hold of packets, and it now carries the tolerances `τ`
and `κ`. (R4) *Mostly there.* UD-142 gives, for one moving clock packet at momentum `p₀` on
the ring, a count `N` at the horizon with `|2π N/(Δ T) − √(1 − v²)| ≤ 1/n + Δ`, `v = σ/E₀`
the band velocity of the lower level. What is missing is the same as a statement about the
rate: `|R − √(1 − v²) Δ| ≤ κ Δ` with `κ` of the order of `Δ`, an inequality between two
real functions of `p₀` and `Δ`. Small. It makes `R₀ = Δ`, the gap, which is `A`'s rate only
if `A` is at rest. (R2, `A` at rest.) *Partly covered since 2026-11-15.* The three
narrow-packet theorems (`narrow_clock_packet_ticks`, `narrow_clock_packet_swing`,
`narrow_clock_packet_swing_fine`) asked `0 < p₀` and now ask `0 ≤ p₀`: the hypothesis was
used only through `|p₀| = p₀`, and the windows of modes were already allowed to start below
zero. So a narrow clock packet at reference momentum `0` ticks, with `v = 0` in the bound.
Not covered: the flat packet of UD-143 to UD-162 is built at `p₀ = 1/2` (the "clock at
rest" there moves at about `0.45`), and its window, localisation and budget theorems have
not been restated for a window centred on the mode `0` (done the same day, on the ring:
UD-172). (R2, the wall.) *Since 2026-11-15, UD-173, for one Dirac carrier; before that:
one lemma, parked.*
`arenaObs_sub_mirror_le` (a parked local file, `UnifiedMirror.lean`, not in the repository): a state minus its
mirror image occupies a mirror-symmetric projection at most twice as much as the state. The
chain with two walls is the mirror-odd sector of the ring (UA-18 (a)); by
`arenaObs_coincProj_prodRay` the weight of a coincidence projector on a product ray is a sum
of products of single-carrier site weights, so what is needed is single-carrier site weights
of "packet minus mirror packet", from UD-143 and BACKLOG ~~#265~~. Not built: the chain's
energy as that sector, and that a coincidence projector of the chain is the restriction of a
mirror-symmetric one. (R2, two levels.) A clock packet's two levels move at different band
velocities; UD-143 already tracks both. (R3) *Not started, and it constrains the design.*
The four times are to be defined from band velocities: `B` at `v`, the messenger at its own
velocity, which is below one for a messenger with rest energy (`1 − 1/(2k²)` in UD-154); the
shortfall goes into the tolerances `τ`. The gates close when packets of finite width
separate, so each `τ` is at least a packet width over a relative velocity. The errors of
UD-164 are absolute, so M6 with errors that are small relative to the readings needs a
family in which the readings grow faster than the widths, as UD-154 did for one reading.
(R5) The family of UD-154 closes its gate over many ticks and fails the conditions of
UD-159. A hand estimate (not Lean) says a family with a slower clock meets them: on the ring
of length `2π K`, a closing lasts about `4 δ K` over the relative velocity, so UD-160 asks
roughly `Δ δ K ≤ 1/150`, while a reading of `k` ticks asks `Δ K` of the order of `k`, hence
`δ` of the order of `1/(150 k)`, and the budgets then ask `N` of the order of `10⁹ k³`. The
errors stay a fixed number of ticks, so they are of relative size `1/k`. **This estimate is
the cheapest test of whether the arrangement can meet M6 at all, and it is to be made a
theorem, for two carriers on the ring where the tools exist, before R2 is built on the
chain.** If it fails, the pointer arrangement has to change and R2 would have been wasted.

Exact scope of UD-165 and UD-166 (BACKLOG ~~#403~~; the test named in the scoping above).
What they give. Values, along a family, that meet every hypothesis of UD-162 together: a run
in which the messenger leaves the clock, goes once round the ring and comes back, the
emission gate closes within a small part of a tick, the conditions of UD-159 hold, and the
return count is within eight ticks of the time away, which is at least `(5/2) k` in the
clock's phase. So the pointer arrangement of UA-18 can give a return reading with an error
that is a fixed number of ticks: the test is passed. What they do not give. (i) Any item of
the list under UD-164 on the arena of UA-18: this is two carriers on a ring, a lap stands in
for the reflection at a wall, the clock moves at about `0.45`, and there is no second
observer. (ii) A late emission: the messenger starts with the clock and the gate begins to
close at the flow time `0`, so the phase condition of UD-159 holds because `cos 0 = 1`; a run
whose emission comes later in the clock's phase is not exhibited. For M6 this is enough only
if `B` emits at its start, which `radar_lorentz_error` allows. (iii) A window for the return:
it is read at the one flow time `lapT4`. (iv) A fast messenger: its rest energy is `1/2` and
its velocity about `0.7`; with the tolerances of UD-164 a slower messenger costs accuracy,
and a family with the velocity near one and a quickly closing gate together is not
exhibited. They pass no gate, and UO-16 is not met. Assumptions used: as UD-152.

Exact scope of UD-167 and UD-168 (BACKLOG ~~#404~~; item R1 of the list under UD-164, first
half). What they give. The run of UD-164 is now the flow of the five-pointer arena of UA-18
(d), solved on the fibres where all five profiles vanish; the five displacements are five
named integrals along the carrier's uncoupled orbit; and UD-164's conclusion holds for the
counts of that flow's five pointers when those integrals have the five histories. What they
do not give. (i) The second half of R1: that the integrals have the histories. For a carrier
that is the product of a messenger and a composite of the two clocks, a gate between any two
of the three is a `gateOp` of UD-140 (with the site map of the composite reading the site of
one clock, or of both), and its `freeShift` from a product ray is the `coincShift` of UD-145,
for which UD-148, UD-157 and UD-159 give the histories from where the carriers are. That
identification and those five applications are not made here. (ii) Anything about three
carriers, the chain, the walls or coincidence gates: the carrier and the five observables
are arbitrary. (iii) R2 to R7. They pass no gate, and UO-16 is not met. Assumptions used:
UA-13 (a), (b), UA-15 (the arena), UA-18 (d).

Exact scope of UD-169 and UD-170 (BACKLOG ~~#405~~; item R1 of the list under UD-164, second
half). What they give. The run of UD-164 is now the five-pointer flow of three carriers, a
messenger and two clocks, with the five gates of UA-18 (c), from a product ray; and each of
the five histories it asks for is a history of a two-carrier gate displacement, the object
that UD-148, UD-157 and UD-159 are about. So R1 is reduced to five two-carrier statements.
What they do not give. (i) The five histories. UD-148, UD-157 and UD-159 give histories from
where two carriers are; they have been applied only on the ring, to a flat messenger and a
flat clock packet (UD-162, UD-165), and three of the five gates here have a clock, not the
messenger, as the carrier whose site opens the gate. The general parts of those theorems
(`abs_coincShift_sub_le`, `coincShift_stop_le`) hold for any first carrier, so a clock can
have that role; no instance with a clock in it exists. On the chain with walls the
histories need R2.
(ii) Any instance: the carriers, their energies, the site maps, the five relations and the
two operators are arbitrary. (iii) R2 to R7. They pass no gate, and UO-16 is not met.
Assumptions used: UA-13 (a), (b), UA-15 (the arena), UA-18 (c), (d).

Exact scope of UD-171 to UD-173 (BACKLOG ~~#406~~, ~~#407~~; items R4 and R2 of the list
under UD-164). What they give. (R4) The hypothesis `hκ` of UD-164 holds with `κ = Δ` when
`R_B` and `R₀` are the level splittings of one two-level clock at a momentum term `s` and at
`0`, `v = s/E₀(s)`. (R2, `A` at rest) A flat clock packet at reference momentum `0` that
ticks at the rate `Δ` and stays within the budget of where it starts, on the ring. (R2, the
wall) For one Dirac carrier, the mirror-odd state of any ring packet: it is odd for all
times, satisfies the boundary condition of UA-18 (a) at the self-mirror sites, and its
weight on a region is controlled by the packet's and the mirror packet's. What they do not
give. (i) R4 for packets: `R_B` in UD-164 is the rate at which a pointer swings, which
UD-142 ties to `ringRate` at the reference momentum; and the `v` of UD-171 is `σ/E₀`, while
the times of UD-164 (R3) are to come from band velocities, which on the lattice differ from
it by the factor `cos(a p₀)`. That mismatch has to be carried as a tolerance in R3; it is
not bounded here. (ii) A clock at rest beside a wall: the rest packet is on the ring, and
its mirror image is not orthogonal to it (UA-18, risk (iii)). (iii) The wall for a clock
carrier (two levels). (iv) That the odd sector is a chain of half the sites with hard ends;
BACKLOG ~~#275~~ (`segOf_diracHam_mulVec`) has that for the even sector. (v) Any reflection:
no window, region or time is instantiated in UD-173. (vi) Any gate, pointer or history:
all three are about uncoupled carriers. They pass no gate, and UO-16 is not met. Assumptions
used: UA-8, UA-12, UA-18 (a).

Exact scope of UD-174 and UD-175 (BACKLOG ~~#408~~; item R2 of the list under UD-164). What
they give. The hypotheses of UD-173's upper bound are discharged from BACKLOG ~~#265~~: the
messenger's wall state is, up to budgets, within the two arcs about its packet's predicted
direction and the mirror direction at every packet time, including while the arcs cross at a
wall; on the folded chain the two arcs are one place, so this is the reflection as a bound
on weights. A two-level clock has a wall state, with the same bound from four budgets. A
clock at rest beside a wall exists and ticks, with every hypothesis discharged: the modes
`±1, …, ±N` with odd amplitudes are a device packet at reference momentum `0`, which UD-142
covers since 2026-11-15. This answers risk (iii) of UA-18: the window without the mode `0`
is orthogonal to its mirror image. What they do not give. (i) Numbers: the budgets are not
evaluated for the wall window, for a moving window or for a mirror window, so no region,
time or width is exhibited. (ii) A moving clock that ticks as a wall state: its window
`W ∪ −W` is not narrow about one momentum, so UD-142 does not apply; the rates at `m` and
`−m` are equal, and the version of UD-142 in the modulus of the momentum is owed. (iii) Any
coincidence weight, gate, pointer or history: the carriers are uncoupled and apart. (iv)
The odd sector as a chain with hard ends. They pass no gate, and UO-16 is not met.
Assumptions used: UA-8, UA-12, UA-18 (a), (b).

Scoping of R7 (2026-11-15; reading, no new Lean). R7 asks for the statements on an open set
of the five pointers' conserved angles, where the pointers act back (UA-18 (g)). What exists.
The five-pointer flow is solved at every fibre (`fiveCoupledFlow_apply`, UD-139): the carrier
moves by the propagator of the energy with the five profile values in it, and each pointer
turns by its profile's derivative times the accumulated reading along that orbit. For two
pointers the distance of that orbit's readings from the uncoupled ones is bounded by the
profile values times the horizon (`abs_arenaObs_regionProj_two_sub_free_le`, UD-109), and the
fibres where that is small are open and of positive measure for `trainF` (`fineFibres`,
`roundFibres`, UD-109, UD-118). What R7 needs. (a) That bound for five pointers and gated
operators: a displacement off the fibres differs from `freeShift` by a term of the order of
the horizon squared times the sum of the profile values, plus the change of the profile's
derivative times the horizon. (b) That the histories of UD-163 survive a small change of the
displacement: `swing` and `closed` have slack by their strict inequalities, and `mid` is a
speed bound that holds for any orbit. (c) The fibre set, open and of positive measure. No
obstruction is known; none of (a) to (c) is built. The set shrinks as the horizon grows, so
R7 is a statement for fixed values (R6), not uniform along a family.

Exact scope of UD-176 and UD-177 (BACKLOG ~~#409~~; item R2 of the list under UD-164). What
they give. Numbers for the budgets left open by UD-174 and UD-175: for every flat window of
`N` neighbouring modes and its mirror the bound is `4 (1/N + 1/(20000 Δ n N))` over `1 − γ`,
up to the horizon `4π n/Δ`. So the clock at rest beside the wall is shown to stay there, and
both observers' clocks tick as wall states: `A` at reference momentum `0` (UD-175), `B` at
`1/2` (UD-177), each with at least `n` ticks and no hypothesis but the sizes. What they do
not give. (i) A region: every bound is for all regions meeting the cosine conditions, and
none is exhibited; the bound is useful for arcs wide against `1/√N`. (ii) The swing of a
wall clock with a margin (the hypothesis of UD-148 and UD-159 about the clock's own
reading): UD-151 and UD-159 are stated for a window about one momentum and were not
generalised with UD-142. (iii) Coincidence weights between wall states. On the ring a
coincidence relation has to be mirror-symmetric (near, or near the mirror site) to be the
chain's; that relation and the gate bounds for it are not built. (iv) The five histories of
UD-170, R3, R6, R7. They pass no gate, and UO-16 is not met. Assumptions used: UA-8, UA-12,
UA-18 (a), (b).

Exact scope of UD-178 (BACKLOG ~~#410~~; items R1 and R2 of the list under UD-164). What it
gives. The coincidence relation that a gate on the walled chain needs when the chain is the
mirror-odd sector of the ring, and the two bounds that turn "where the two carriers are"
into "the gate is open" and "the gate is closed" for it, for any two rays. The weights
outside the double arcs are the quantities UD-176 bounds for wall states. What it does not
give. (i) The combination: UD-176 is a bound on weights of state vectors at packet times and
UD-178 takes occupancies along uncoupled orbits on the arena; UD-173 links the two for the
messenger, and for the clock the link of UD-149 is for a device ray and has not been applied
to a wall state. So no gate is shown open or closed for any state. (ii) Times: the
conditions are on angles; no flow time at which they hold is exhibited. (iii) A gate between
two clocks. (iv) The histories of UD-170, which also need the wall clock's swing with a
margin. It passes no gate, and UO-16 is not met. Assumptions used: UA-18 (a), (c).

Exact scope of UD-179 to UD-181 (BACKLOG ~~#411~~, ~~#412~~; items R1, R2 and R3 of the list
under UD-164). What they give. (R1, R2) For a messenger and one clock on the walled chain,
both as wall states with numbers: the two hypotheses of UD-148 about where the carriers are
(`wall_gate_open`, `wall_gate_closed`), and the hypothesis about the clock's own reading
(the swing with a margin), for the clock at rest beside the wall and for the moving clock.
(R3) The four times defined, with the two relations of UD-164 proved, so those relations
are no longer hypotheses of a run with these times; and the least tolerances a messenger of
speed `u < 1` forces. What they do not give, and one thing they show. (i) **The messenger
built so far is too slow for M6.** The tolerances of UD-181 are of relative size
`(1 − u)/u`, to be small against the dilation `v²/2`; the wall states of UD-176 and UD-179
have `μ ≥ 1` and `p ≤ 1/2`, so `u ≤ 0.45`. By `one_sub_vRel_le` a messenger needs `μ²/p²`
small against `v²`; its budgets (UD-176 asks `μ ≥ 1`) grow like `1/μ` and are not built.
For this reason the five histories of UD-170 were not assembled on the present messenger.
(ii) Flow times: the gate conditions are on angles, and that the gates close at flow times
within stated tolerances of the times of UD-181 is not shown. (iii) The gate between the two
clocks. (iv) The lattice's band velocity against the velocity of UD-181; the clock's two
levels. (v) R6, R7. They pass no gate, and UO-16 is not met. Assumptions used: UA-18 (a),
(b), (c).

Exact scope of UD-182 and UD-183 (BACKLOG ~~#413~~, ~~#414~~; items R2 and R3 of the list
under UD-164). What they give. (R2) The messenger that UD-181 asks for: rest energy
`0 < μ ≤ 1`, velocity within `2μ²` of `1` at the momentum `1/2`, with its budget, wall
state and wall gate, the price of the speed explicit in `lightBudget`. (R3) The conditions
on angles of UD-179 and UD-182 as intervals of the flow time for carriers on straight
lines: the emission gate is open up to `(w − 2δ)/(u − v)`, closed from `(w + 2δ)/(u − v)`
until `(w + 2δ)/(u + v)` before the return of UD-181, and open again within
`(w − 2δ)/(u + v)` of it; and the gate at the return, where the messenger meets the clock's
mirror image, is shown for the wall states. What they do not give. (i) Values: no choice of
`n`, `N`, `μ`, `Δ`, `δ`, `w`, `d` is shown to make `lightBudget` small against `1 − cos δ`,
the closing interval `4δ/(u − v)` short against a tick, and `μ²` small against `v²`
together. That is R6 and is the test of whether the tolerances close. (ii) The clock's two
levels: UD-183 is for one pair of speeds. (iii) One bound joining the intervals of UD-183 to
the tolerances `τ` of UD-164. (iv) The gate between the two clocks; the five histories of
UD-170. (v) The lattice's band velocity. (vi) R7. They pass no gate, and UO-16 is not met.
Assumptions used: UA-18 (a), (b), (c).

Exact scope of UD-184 and UD-185 (BACKLOG ~~#415~~; items R6 and R1 of the list under
UD-164). What they give. An answer to the question left by UD-182: along a family in `k`
the messenger's and the clock's bounds, the gate's closing interval and the messenger's
slowness are small together, with the gate's weight at most `1/(1000 k)`, the closing at
most `1/1000` of a phase, and `1 − u ≤ 2/k²`. The clock's bound had to be restated at each
packet time: at the end of the horizon of UD-176 it is too coarse. And the gate between the
two clocks for any two clock rays. What they do not give. (i) Readings. The test is of the
bounds that the gate theorems give; UD-148, UD-157 and UD-159 have size conditions of their
own on the swing's margin, on `|z| ε t` and on the band, and those are not checked for this
family. UD-165 and UD-166 checked the analogous conditions on the ring and they held; that
is evidence, not a proof, for the walled chain. (ii) That the relative angular speed is at
least `1/(20 K)` and the run at most `10 K`: taken as given; for `k ≥ 2` the messenger is
at least at `1/2` and the clock's lower level at about `0.447` (a calculation, not in
Lean), a small margin. A larger margin needs a slower clock or `k` larger. (iii) The
comparison with the dilation: the family has `Δ → 0` and about `k` ticks in a run, so the
count's own error of a fixed number of ticks is `O(1/k)` of the run; that this and `2/k²`
are small against `v²/2` is not stated. (iv) The five histories of UD-170; the clock's two
levels; the band velocity; R7. They pass no gate, and UO-16 is not met. Assumptions used:
UA-18 (a), (b), (c).

Exact scope of UD-186 (BACKLOG ~~#416~~; Task 3 of the research delivery plan). What it
gives. The wall is a property of the energy: the mirror-odd sector of the ring, with the
ring's evolution, is unitarily the chain on `{0, …, M}` with hard ends, with an explicit
energy. UA-18 (a) chose the boundary condition "the upper component vanishes at each wall,
the end hop is `√2` times the bulk hop"; both are now theorems (`odd_apply_eq_zero`,
`oddSegOf_end`, and `segHop` of BACKLOG ~~#275~~), so nothing downstream needs correcting.
The weights that UD-173 to UD-185 bound for mirror-symmetric regions are weights of regions
of that chain. What it does not give. (i) The two-level clock on the segment: each of its
two blocks is covered, the device as one carrier is not restated. (ii) The gates and
pointers on the segment: the coincidence relation `wallNear` is mirror-symmetric, which is
what makes it a relation of the chain, but the coupled flow is not carried over. (iii) That
`segHam` is a Hermitian matrix; it is symmetric on the states the chain has. (iv) Rings
with an odd number of sites. It passes no gate, and UO-16 is not met. Assumptions used:
UA-8, UA-18 (a).

Exact scope of UD-187 and UD-188 (BACKLOG ~~#417~~, ~~#418~~; Tasks 5 and 4 of the research
delivery plan). What they give. (Task 5) The observer at rest can be put at the far wall:
its clock swings as before and stays in the arc about the place it was put. The rate
hypothesis of UD-164 is a theorem with the velocity that the packets' predicted positions
move at, `κ = Δ + a² p³/6`; the velocity of the rate and the velocity of the kinematics are
now the same number. (Task 4) The gate windows for the whole chain (`d` up to a full turn in
the sum of the angles), for a carrier arriving at an observer who sits at a wall (the relay
and the meeting), and the three timing tolerances of UD-164 as `ρ` plus the messenger's
lateness. What they do not give. (i) The windows for the actual wall states: UD-188 is
arithmetic of straight motion; joining it to UD-179, UD-182 and UD-183 with the clock's two
levels intersected is the histories' work. (ii) The count theorem for the translated clock.
(iii) The gate between the messenger and the translated clock, or between the two clocks,
for their wall states. (iv) The emission's tolerance, which is the first gate window
itself. (v) The histories, R6 for readings, R7. They pass no gate, and UO-16 is not met.
Assumptions used: UA-8, UA-12, UA-18 (a), (b).

Exact scope of UD-189 (BACKLOG ~~#419~~; Task 6 of the research delivery plan, first part).
What it gives. The two kinds of history that UD-170 asks for, as consequences of three
kinds of fact about two carriers: where they are (weights inside and outside the relation
on stated intervals), the clock's own displacement (its swing), and sizes. These are the
facts that UD-179, UD-182, UD-183, UD-185 and UD-187 supply for wall states and that UD-180
and UD-187 supply for the swings. So each hypothesis of `tri_radar_lorentz` about a pointer
is now one application away from statements that exist, for a messenger and a clock; for
the two clocks the gate exists for any rays (UD-185) and its hypotheses for wall states do
not. What it does not give. (i) Any history of the walled chain: the theorem is not
applied. (ii) The band of the away history (Task 7): a hypothesis on where the `r`-gated
pointer stopped. (iii) The intervals: the flow times `t_a`, `t_b`, `t₃`, `t₄`, `t₅` at which
the weights are small, for the wall states and both levels of the clock. (iv) The sizes
for a family (Task 8); R7. It passes no gate, and UO-16 is not met. Assumptions used:
UA-13, UA-17, UA-18 (c).

Exact scope of UD-190 (BACKLOG ~~#420~~; Tasks 6 and 7 of the research delivery plan, for
the moving observer). What it gives. Two of the five hypotheses of `tri_radar_lorentz`
about pointers, `He` and `Hr`, as theorems for the wall states: the emission pointer's
displacement is a gated history and the return pointer's is an away history, for the light
messenger and the moving clock on the walled chain, with one strength for both. The band of
the away history (R5), a hypothesis of UD-189, is derived: the emission is at the start, so
the emission pointer stops within the stated margin of `0`, and that is inside the band.
What it does not give. (i) The intervals: the conditions on predicted angles on `[0, t_a]`,
`[t_b, t₃]`, `[t₄, t₅]` are hypotheses; UD-183 and UD-188 give them for straight motion at
one speed, and they have not been joined for the two clock levels and the packet-time
offsets. (ii) The sizes for a family: `wall_values` (UD-184) gives the gate's bound and
the closing interval; the band's inequality and the two `1/6` conditions are not checked
for it. (iii) The three histories that involve the observer at rest (the relay and the two
meeting pointers). (iv) A later emission. (v) Counts: they follow from UD-163 once the run
is the five-pointer flow. (vi) R7. It passes no gate, and UO-16 is not met. Assumptions
used: UA-12, UA-13, UA-17, UA-18.

Exact scope of UD-191 and UD-192 (BACKLOG ~~#421~~, ~~#422~~; Task 6 of the research
delivery plan). What they give. With UD-190, each of the five hypotheses of
`tri_radar_lorentz` about a pointer (`He`, `Hr`, `Hμ`, `Hρ`, `Hm`) is a theorem for the
wall states on the walled chain: the light messenger, the moving clock, and the clock at
rest translated to its place. Each is proved from where the carriers are, the clock's swing,
and sizes. What they do not give. (i) The five theorems are separate: each asserts a
strength for its own pointer and takes its own interval and size hypotheses. One statement
for one prepared state of three carriers, with one strength per clock, is not written; the
three strengths on the moving clock and the two on the clock at rest are each the one the
swing theorem provides, so they can be taken equal, which is not stated. (ii) The
intervals: every condition on predicted angles is a hypothesis. UD-183 and UD-188 give them
for straight motion at one speed; they are not joined for the clock's two levels and the
packet-time offsets. (iii) The sizes for a family: `wall_values` covers the bounds of one
gate only. (iv) `tri_radar_lorentz` is therefore not yet applied, and no count or Lorentz
statement about the walled chain is proved. (v) R7. They pass no gate, and UO-16 is not
met. Assumptions used: UA-12, UA-13, UA-17, UA-18.

Exact scope of UD-193 and UD-194 (BACKLOG ~~#423~~, ~~#424~~; Tasks 4, 6 and 10 of the
research delivery plan). What they give. UD-193 is the first statement about the walled
chain in which counts of the five-pointer flow satisfy the Lorentz statement: one prepared
state of three carriers, one strength per clock, the hypotheses of UD-170 about pointers
all discharged by UD-190 to UD-192. To make that possible the strength and the clock's
swing bound became hypotheses of UD-190 to UD-192, and the emission condition of UD-190
took the mirror form. UD-194 reduces the seven conditions on predicted angles, each over an
interval, to thirteen inequalities between the rates and the ends of the intervals. What
they do not give. (i) UD-193 is conditional on the seven angle conditions and on the sizes;
UD-194 is arithmetic and is not yet applied to it. `radar_gate_times` shows the thirteen
inequalities can be met together, by explicit times, under six conditions on the rates, the
start and the half-angle; those six and the sizes are not checked for a family (Task 8). (ii) The errors of UD-193
are not shown small. They contain the difference of each gate time from a kinematic time.
UD-194 found that the start is forced to be a mirror coincidence: the moving clock is
prepared before the wall and moving into it, is reflected at the flow time `τ = π/(2 R_B)`,
and only then sets out. Against the times of UD-181 for the distance `π` the relay is early
by `b₁ τ/a` and the meeting late by `τ`, a quarter of a tick. On paper that is of relative
size `O(1/k)` for the family of the feasibility note, the order of the tick discretisation;
it is not a theorem. (iii) The fibres where the five profiles vanish only: R7 is untouched.
(iv) They pass no gate, and UO-16 is not met. Assumptions used: UA-12, UA-13, UA-17, UA-18.

Exact scope of UD-195 (BACKLOG ~~#425~~; Task 8 of the research delivery plan). What it
gives. For the family of the feasibility note with `k ≥ 100`: the six conditions under
which UD-194 gives gate times, and every size condition of UD-193 in a form from which it
follows (each gate time is at most `8K`, each horizon at least `8K`, each closing term at
most `16/2500`, the band). With `wall_values` (UD-184) for the budget, every hypothesis of
UD-193 is then a consequence of proved inequalities for this family. What it does not
give. (i) UD-193 is not applied in this entry: the join is a separate step. (ii) The error
of UD-193 is not bounded. (iii) Nothing about the flow, and nothing off the fibres where
the profiles vanish. It passes no gate, and UO-16 is not met. Assumptions used: none beyond
real arithmetic and the definitions of UA-18's arena.

Exact scope of UD-196 (BACKLOG ~~#426~~; Tasks 8 and 10 of the research delivery plan, on
the fibres where the profiles vanish). What it gives. One theorem about one family of
prepared states of three carriers on the walled chain, in which no hypothesis about angles,
sizes, histories, light speed, the dilation law or the radar relations is left: the
hypotheses are `k ≥ 100`, the size of the ring and that its number of sites is even. Its
conclusion is that the five tick pointers of the five-pointer flow have counts in five
windows of flow time and that those counts satisfy the Lorentz statement of UD-164 for the
velocity `vRel 1 1 (1/2)` within `150 K/k` in the flow time. The run lasts about `πK/v ≈ 7K`
and holds about `1.1 k` ticks, so the error is about `21/k` of the run and about `24` ticks:
it vanishes along the family while the distance (the whole chain) and the velocity are
fixed. What it does not give. (i) R7: the statement is on the fibres where the five
profiles vanish, a set of measure zero in the pointer angles, where the carriers move
freely. Until it holds on a non-empty open set of pointer angles the result does not meet
the plan's definition of success. (ii) The comparison with the length of the run is
arithmetic on paper. (iii) One velocity and one distance. (iv) The emission is a
coincidence with the moving clock's mirror image, and the kinematic times are those of
UD-181 for the distance `π`; the shifts caused by the start (UD-194) are inside the error.
(v) It passes no gate, and UO-16 is not met. Assumptions used: UA-12, UA-13, UA-17, UA-18.

Exact scope of UD-197 (BACKLOG ~~#427~~; Task 9 of the research delivery plan, first
part). What it gives. The two kinds of history that UD-164 takes survive a perturbation of
the displacement that starts close and drifts slowly, with explicit new errors. What it does
not give. It is one of five pieces of R7 and says nothing about the flow. Still owed: (i)
the five-pointer flow on an arbitrary fibre (the conserved angles are constants, so the
carrier moves by the free flow of the matrix `H + Σ f_i(θ_i) G_i` and pointer `i` is turned
by `f_i'(θ_i)` times the accumulated expectation of `G_i` along it; the repository has this
only where the profiles vanish); (ii) the bound on how far that orbit's expectations are
from the uncoupled ones, hence the drift rate `λ`; (iii) the true closing bounds `c₀`, `c₁`
of the wall histories (the structures of UD-163 keep only `1/6`); (iv) counts for a start
angle near `1/12`; (v) the open set for the family, and the join with UD-196. It passes no
gate, and UO-16 is not met. Assumptions used: none.

Exact scope of UD-198 (BACKLOG ~~#428~~; Task 9 of the research delivery plan, second and
third parts). What it gives. Pieces (i) and (ii) of the list under UD-197: the flow off the
fibres where the profiles vanish is the free flow of one constant matrix, exactly; and a
pointer's displacement there is a drift of the uncoupled one at an explicit rate, which is
the hypothesis of UD-197. What it does not give. Still owed for R7: (iii) the true closing
bounds of the wall histories; (iv) counts for a start angle near `1/12`; (v) the open set
for the family: the rate must be made small by the choice of angles (it carries the factor
`T ≈ 8K`, so the neighbourhood shrinks with `k` and with the norms of the five gates), the
five gates must be shown Hermitian, and the result joined with UD-196. It passes no gate,
and UO-16 is not met. Assumptions used: UA-12, UA-13, UA-18.

Exact scope of UD-199 (BACKLOG ~~#429~~; Task 9 of the research delivery plan, fourth
part). What it gives. The top-down theorem of UD-168 without its two restrictions: any
conserved angles and any start of the moving angles. With UD-197 and UD-198 the route to an
open set is now a chain of proved steps, each general. What it does not give. The chain is
not closed for the walled chain. Still owed: (i) the five histories of the uncoupled
displacements as an exported statement (they are inside the proof of UD-193) and their true
closing bounds, from the gate weights of UD-190 to UD-192; (ii) the three remaining gates
Hermitian; (iii) the profile `g · trainF` and its derivative near its zero; (iv) the open
set itself for the family, with radii depending on `k`, the size of the ring and the norms
of the gates, and the arithmetic that the enlarged errors still fit; (v) the join with
UD-196. It passes no gate, and UO-16 is not met. Assumptions used: UA-12, UA-13, UA-18.

Exact scope of UD-200 and UD-201 (BACKLOG ~~#430~~, ~~#431~~; Tasks 9 and 10 of the research
delivery plan). What they give. Items (i) to (v) of the list under UD-199: the closing bounds
(`coincShift_closed_le`, used with the gate weights of UD-190 to UD-192 inside
`tri_wall_radar_lorentz_open`; the five uncoupled histories are consumed there, not exported
under a name of their own), the three gates Hermitian, the profile near its zero, the radius,
and the join with UD-196. The theorem under the plan's name,
`csd_operational_lorentz_from_records`, has no hypothesis on histories, on the messenger's
speed, on a dilation law or on radar relations; its error is `151/(π k)` of the distance
`π K`, which does not shrink; and it holds on an open set of the ten pointer angles containing
the start. What they do not give. (a) The open set is not explicit and nothing bounds its
radius from below as `k` grows: the drift rate carries the flow time `≈ 8K` and the norms of
the gates, so the radius found shrinks along the family. The statement is, for each `k`, an
open set; it is not one open set for the whole family. (b) Nothing about the measure of the
open set. (c) One family, one velocity `vRel 1 1 (1/2)`, one distance (the whole chain),
`1 + 1`. (d) The Lorentz statement is that of UD-164: two components of the boost of the
rest clock's counts against the moving clock's counts, read in flow-time windows; it is not
a metric and not an invariance statement for a class of observers. (e) Tasks 11 (the bridge,
`UnifiedRecordSpacetimeBridge.lean`) and 12 (the audit of the theorem against the test of UO-16) are
not started. Whether UO-16 is met is for that audit and the author to decide; this note does
not declare it. Assumptions used: UA-12, UA-13, UA-18.

Exact scope of UD-202 (BACKLOG ~~#432~~; Tasks 10, 11 and 12 of the research delivery plan).
What it gives. The items of Task 10 that UD-201 did not state: the two observers'
coordinates as named functions of the counts, the comparison of their intervals, and the
positive measure of the open set that Task 9 asks for. The identifications of Task 11: one
point, one Hamiltonian flow, the preserved measure, and what a count is. The audit of Task
12 is `specs/unified-m6-audit.md`. What it does not give. (a) **The interval's residual is
not shown to vanish against the interval.** The bound is `E (2 |b₁| + 2 |b₂| + 2E)` with
`E = 151 K/k`; it is small against `b²` only if the coordinates are of the size of the run,
and no bound on the counts is exported from UD-164 (the bounds `|2πN − R t| ≤ …` are inside
its proof). This is the clause of UO-16 "the residual tends to zero" for the interval, and
it is open. (b) The radius of the open set is still not explicit (Remainder (a) of
~~#431~~). (c) The audit's findings F1 to F4. UO-16 is not marked complete. Assumptions
used: UA-12, UA-13, UA-18.

Exact scope of UD-203 (BACKLOG ~~#433~~; finding F1 of `specs/unified-m6-audit.md`). What
it gives. The clause of UO-16 "two observers assign the same Minkowski interval within
stated errors; the residual tends to zero along a scaling family" for the interval: the
residual is at most `4600/(π² k)` of the squared distance. What it does not give. A lower
bound on the interval itself (the rest observer's time is about `π K (1/v − 1)`, which is
not stated); an explicit open set (F2); anything beyond one family. Whether UO-16 is now
met is the author's decision; this note does not mark it. Assumptions used: UA-12, UA-13,
UA-18.

Exact scope of UD-204 (BACKLOG ~~#434~~). What it gives. The residue of UD-203: the
comparison scale is now the interval itself, and the rest observer's coordinate is tied to
the kinematic time of the family, not only bounded. What it does not give. An explicit open
set (F2, ~~#435~~ if landed); anything beyond one family. Assumptions used: UA-12, UA-13,
UA-18.

**UO-16: marked complete by the author's decision, 2026-11-17**, with the qualifications of
`specs/unified-m6-audit.md` §5 standing as its scope: an operational reconstruction of the
kinematics of the arena's Dirac carriers (Posit 11), for one family, in `1 + 1` dimensions,
with reading windows in the flow time, strengths that exist, and an open set of pointer
angles of positive measure for each `k`.

Exact scope of UD-205 (BACKLOG ~~#435~~; finding F2 of `specs/unified-m6-audit.md`). What
it gives. The open set of the plan's theorem contains an explicit box of radius
`1/(12000 k²)` about the start, the same for every ring. What it does not give. A radius
that does not shrink with `k`; the plan's Task 9 does not ask for one. Assumptions used:
UA-12, UA-13, UA-18.

Exact scope of UD-206 (BACKLOG ~~#294~~; milestone M7, obligation UO-17). What it gives. The
matter of every later row of M7: a Hermitian nearest-neighbour Hamiltonian on a torus of any
number of axes, its upper-band modes as exact eigenvectors, the dispersion
`E² = μ² + Σ_i (2J sin θ_i)²`, and the clock's dilation in the defined speed `c |s|/E`. What
it does not give. Packets (#296); the group velocity and the lattice's preferred directions
(#295); any detector, record or count on this lattice (#297); the `d + 1` statement (#298);
anything on the unified arena. The doublers at `θ_i = π` are not removed. No new assumption.

Exact scope of UD-207 (BACKLOG ~~#295~~; milestone M7, obligation UO-17). What it gives. The
one fact that more than one space axis adds to the lattice: the group speed of a massless
carrier depends on its direction at order `(a p)²`, through `Σ_i n_i⁴` (`1` on an axis, `1/d`
on the diagonal), with an explicit remainder; and a bound, of order `a²`, on how far the
lattice velocity is from commuting with rotations. What it does not give. That a packet's
centre moves at this velocity (#296); any record of it (#297); the expansion of the defect at
`μ > 0`; a sharp constant. Whether the direction dependence is a prediction or an artefact of
the regulator is UO-18's test, not answered here. No new assumption.

Exact scope of UD-208 (BACKLOG ~~#436~~; obligation UO-25, Task 1 of the plan of 2026-11-18).
What it gives. The tick counts of M6 in the SigmaLayer's own terms: each count is a recorded
fact whose event is a measurable set of initial states of the arena, defined by the
Hamiltonian flow of UA-18; membership is the count of the orbit, hence a number of
crossings; the five counts of the radar theorem are a record history whose compatible
region contains the run's start. What it does not give. A preparation: the compatible
region is not shown to have positive measure for the arena's measure, because the radar
theorem fixes the carriers' rays (Task 2). A register: no coordinate of the arena stores a
count, and the outcome region at the reading is defined by following the orbit backward. A
second apparatus that reads the count (Task 5). Any probability (Task 6). No new assumption.

Exact scope of UD-209 (BACKLOG #296, first part; milestone M7, obligation UO-17; Task 8 of the
plan of 2026-11-18). What it gives. A carrier that is not spread over the torus: a packet on
a window of momenta, with one translation phase for each axis, each rotating at its
component of the group velocity of UD-207 within an explicit error that is small for a
narrow window; the packet's norm; a box condition under which the window has no wrap-around.
What it does not give. The weight of the packet outside a box of sites, which is what a
coincidence gate needs. The limit in physical units with errors free of `L`. Flat windows
and their budget. The two-level clock on a packet. Anything for `μ = 0`: the drift bound
carries `1/μ` and is not claimed there. No new assumption.
